In the bilinear sum use $\alpha_\ell(a)=\Theta_\ell(a)\alpha_0(a)$ and $\beta_\ell(b)$. The first phase disappears under Cauchy, and its coefficient norm is unchanged. Restoring the model gives exactly $\theta_\ell(ab)c_*\mu^2(ab)N(ab)^{-1/6}$. All cross-coprimality errors use absolute values. In particular, the three-prime transition still has saving $L^{-3/2}$.

For the integrated estimate, at large $B$ the diagonal remains $A\sqrt B\ll X^{.80}$ and the other term has arbitrary logarithmic saving. At small $B$ partition both norm variables into the same $O(J)$ cells. Their coefficient norms $a_i,b_j$ are unchanged. Near pairs give $X^{5/6}L^{O(1)}H^{-1/6}J^{-1/2}$ as in <a href="#eq:near-cell-diagonal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:near-cell-diagonal">[eq:near-cell-diagonal]</a>; all absolute errors sum to $X^{5/6}L^{O(1)}J^{3/2}\varepsilon_J^{1/2}$ as in <a href="#eq:all-cell-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:all-cell-error">[eq:all-cell-error]</a>. For far pairs, integration by parts differentiates the height cutoff. The angular phases stay in the coefficients, and the common Mellin shifts remain independent of the averaging height. Thus the factor $(J/T)^q$ and the unrestricted-shift use of dispersion are preserved. With $J=L^{D'}$ or $X^\gamma$ the same positive gaps prove the integrated estimate. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-38"></a>
#### The exact decomposition and sharp cutoff

We finally verify that these interfaces prove Theorem <a href="#thm:angular" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:angular">23</a>. The identities of Section <a href="#sec:decomposition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:decomposition">6</a> apply to both squarefree-supported kernels $G_\ell$ and $\mathcal D_\ell$. The prime detector, distinguished tuples and semiprime subtraction are scalar identities. Every stopping predicate depends on norms alone. For a fixed stopping bin and its counts, multiplicativity allocates the phase to independent coefficients $\Theta_\ell(a)\alpha(a)$ and $\Theta_\ell(b)\beta(b)$; the binomial weight is unchanged. The permitted overlaps are still annihilated by the separate squarefree restriction on each kernel. There is no new coupled cutoff or height dependence in the coefficients.

No-stop terms have an angular inner variable and use Proposition <a href="#prop:angular-typeI" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:angular-typeI">26</a>. Every sparse support count, absolute coefficient moment, Rankin bound and general-coefficient cubic-sieve application is unchanged. For $B>X^{.40}$ the coefficient is precisely $\Theta_\ell\beta_0$ with the original independently smooth prime weights; Proposition <a href="#prop:angular-dual" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:angular-dual">27</a> therefore applies. Lemma <a href="#lem:angular-sw" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:angular-sw">28</a> proves the required sequence condition. The number of pieces, transition indices and sparse remainders is exactly the one already counted.

Fix $\ell$ first. Choose the absolute small-$B$ $\gamma$, then $\kappa,\rho$ as in <a href="#eq:power-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:power-parameters">[eq:power-parameters]</a>, then $\xi$ and the fixed tuple complexity. Subsequent neighborhood sizes, logarithmic savings and constants may depend on $\ell$. They are chosen in the order of Section <a href="#sec:perron" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:perron">7</a>. There is no dependence returning from an angular exceptional moment to the absolute $\gamma$.

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-angular-dispersion-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-angular-dispersion</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:angular" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:angular">23</a>.* Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:perron-truncation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:perron-truncation">22</a> to the prime coefficients with their angular factors and $T_{\max}=X^{1/6+\rho}$. Equal-norm collection has the same absolute divisor bound, so the truncation error is $O_\epsilon(X^\epsilon(1+X/T_{\max}))$ and is $o(X^{5/6}/\log X)$ for $\epsilon<\rho$. At low heights the exact decomposition just verified, the angular Type I comparison and the angular bilinear comparison give the same arbitrary logarithmic savings. The three-prime transition contributes $O_\ell(X^{5/6}L^{-3/2}(1+\log L)^C)$, which is also little-oh at the required scale. Sparse terms retain their power savings.

At high heights first bound the undecomposed angular prime model. If $d_{\ell,n}$ is its coefficient after collecting equal norms, then $\sum_n|d_{\ell,n}|^2\ll X^{2/3}L^{O(1)}$ by absolute divisor multiplicity. The ordinary mean value therefore gives the same $T^{-1/2}$ saving used in Section <a href="#sec:perron" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:perron">7</a>. This step does not use angular prime cancellation. For the Gauss-sum term the integrated bilinear estimate is Proposition <a href="#prop:angular-dispersion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:angular-dispersion">29</a>. The high-height Type I estimate gives the same two endpoint powers, $.805+\epsilon$ and $5/6-3\kappa/8+\rho/2+\epsilon$. Choose $\epsilon$ below the two fixed gaps to $5/6$. Its pole term is absent unless $\ell=0$, when its arbitrary height decay is the original one. The two-cell and far-pair savings proved above pay for the same polynomial number of pieces and the $O(L)$ height dyads. Hence the dyadic prime comparison <a href="#eq:prime-dyadic-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prime-dyadic-comparison">[eq:prime-dyadic-comparison]</a> holds with $\theta_\ell(\pi)$ inserted. Geometric dyadic summation gives <a href="#eq:angular-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:angular-comparison">[eq:angular-comparison]</a>.

For $\ell\ne0$, take $\chi$ principal in Lemma <a href="#lem:angular-hecke" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:angular-hecke">24</a> and remove the prime logarithm by partial summation. Thus $P_\ell(y)=\sum_{N\pi\le y}\theta_\ell(\pi)
 \ll_{\ell,M}y(\log y)^{-M}$ for every fixed $M$. Abel summation now gives $$\sum_{N\pi\le X}\theta_\ell(\pi)(N\pi)^{-1/6}
 =X^{-1/6}P_\ell(X)
       +\frac16\int_2^X P_\ell(y)y^{-7/6}\,dy
 =o_\ell(X^{5/6}/\log X).$$ The lower endpoint contributes only a bounded term. Combining this with <a href="#eq:angular-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:angular-comparison">[eq:angular-comparison]</a> proves <a href="#eq:angular-cancellation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:angular-cancellation">[eq:angular-cancellation]</a>. ◻

</details>

For $\ell=0$ the model is the radial one and retains coefficient $(6/5)c_*$. Inert primes still have total size $O(\sqrt X)$. The rational half-sum consequence <a href="#eq:rational-main" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rational-main">[eq:rational-main]</a> is unchanged. For $\ell\ne0$ a conjugate pair instead contributes $2\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(\theta_\ell(\pi)G(\pi))$; no identification with the untwisted rational Kummer summand is asserted.

<a id="pm-h-39"></a>
#### Fixed powers of the Gauss-sum values

<a id="eq:gauss-power-cancellation"></a>
The angular result also determines the fixed power moments whose exponents are not divisible by three. In the normalization <a href="#eq:gauss-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gauss-definition">[eq:gauss-definition]</a>, the cubic Gauss-sum identity <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[DR2024](#ref-DR2024), Equation (1.8)] gives $$G(\pi)^3=-\frac{\pi}{|\pi|}=-\theta_1(\pi),
 \qquad |G(\pi)|=1.$$ Consequently, for every fixed integer $k$ with $3\nmid k$, $$\label{eq:gauss-power-cancellation}
 \sum_{\substack{N\pi\le X\\\pi\equiv1\pmod3\ \mathrm{prime}}}
       G(\pi)^k
 =\boldsymbol1_{k\in\{-1,1\}}\frac65c_*
       \frac{X^{5/6}}{\log X}
   +o_k\!\left(\frac{X^{5/6}}{\log X}\right).$$ Indeed, for $k=3m+1$ and $k=3m-1$, respectively, $$G(\pi)^{3m+1}=(-1)^m\theta_m(\pi)G(\pi),
 \qquad
 G(\pi)^{3m-1}=(-1)^m
                  \overline{\theta_{-m}(\pi)G(\pi)}.$$ For $m\ne0$, Theorem <a href="#thm:angular" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:angular">23</a> gives cancellation; for $m=0$, Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> and its complex conjugate give the same real main term. Negative powers are well-defined because $|G(\pi)|=1$.

Thus <a href="#eq:gauss-power-cancellation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gauss-power-cancellation">[eq:gauss-power-cancellation]</a> proves the fixed-power cases $3\nmid k$, $|k|>1$, of the complementary conjecture in <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[DR2024](#ref-DR2024), Equation (1.9)]. For a nonzero multiple $k=3m$, the same identity instead gives $G(\pi)^k=(-1)^m\theta_m(\pi)$. The Hecke prime estimates used here do not bound these prime-angle sums at the stronger scale $X^{5/6}/\log X$, so those cases remain outside the conclusion.

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "The proof of the sharp first moment. Solid arrows indicate uses of proved estimates. The exact prime decomposition supplies the coefficient classes required by both bilinear branches.",
      "figure_id": "fig:proof-map",
      "pdf_page": 4,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:proof-map",
          "exact_quote": "The proof of the sharp first moment. Solid arrows indicate\nuses of proved estimates. The exact prime decomposition supplies the\ncoefficient classes required by both bilinear branches.",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1cm,y=1cm,\n box/.style={draw,rounded corners=2pt,align=center,font=\\small,\n             text width=3.35cm,minimum height=1.05cm,inner sep=5pt},\n arrow/.style={->,>=stealth,semithick}]\n\\node[box] (level) at (-4,3.4) {Voronoi formula\\\\Height mean square};\n\\node[box] (moments) at (0,3.4) {Structured moments\\\\Section~\\ref{sec:dual}};\n\\node[box] (decomp) at (4,3.4) {Prime decomposition\\\\Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}};\n\\node[box] (typei) at (-4,1.6) {Type~I estimates\\\\Section~\\ref{sec:typeI}};\n\\node[box] (low) at (0,1.6) {Dispersion estimates\\\\Section~\\ref{sec:dispersion}};\n\\node[box] (high) at (4,1.6) {Two-variable localization\\\\Section~\\ref{sec:perron}};\n\\node[box,text width=6cm] (end) at (0,-.3) {Sharp cutoff and prime model\\\\Theorem~\\ref{thm:main}};\n\\draw[arrow] (level)--(typei);\n\\draw[arrow] (moments)--(low);\n\\draw[arrow] (typei)--(low);\n\\draw[arrow] (decomp)--(high);\n\\draw[arrow] (decomp.south west)--(low.north east);\n\\draw[arrow] (low)--(high);\n\\draw[arrow] (typei.south)--(end.north west);\n\\draw[arrow] (low)--(end);\n\\draw[arrow] (high.south)--(end.north east);\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A directed graph / flowchart with 7 rectangular nodes arranged in three horizontal tiers.\nTop tier (3 nodes):\n- Node: \"Voronoi formula \\n Height mean square\"\n- Node: \"Structured moments \\n Section 4\"\n- Node: \"Prime decomposition \\n Section 6\"\nMiddle tier (3 nodes):\n- Node: \"Type I estimates \\n Section 3\"\n- Node: \"Dispersion estimates \\n Section 5\"\n- Node: \"Two-variable \\n localization \\n Section 7\"\nBottom tier (1 node):\n- Node: \"Sharp cutoff and prime model \\n Theorem 1.1\"\nEdges (solid arrows):\n- From \"Voronoi formula \\n Height mean square\" straight down to \"Type I estimates \\n Section 3\"\n- From \"Structured moments \\n Section 4\" straight down to \"Dispersion estimates \\n Section 5\"\n- From \"Prime decomposition \\n Section 6\" diagonally down-left to \"Dispersion estimates \\n Section 5\"\n- From \"Prime decomposition \\n Section 6\" straight down to \"Two-variable \\n localization \\n Section 7\"\n- From \"Type I estimates \\n Section 3\" horizontally right to \"Dispersion estimates \\n Section 5\"\n- From \"Type I estimates \\n Section 3\" diagonally down-right to \"Sharp cutoff and prime model \\n Theorem 1.1\"\n- From \"Dispersion estimates \\n Section 5\" straight down to \"Sharp cutoff and prime model \\n Theorem 1.1\"\n- From \"Two-variable \\n localization \\n Section 7\" diagonally down-left to \"Sharp cutoff and prime model \\n Theorem 1.1\""
    }
